Volume 104 / Issue 198 / October 15, 2026

News

Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Prices supported by US crude stock draw to lowest level since July
US crude stocks drop 8 million barrels
Gulf Coast inventories hit July lows
Refinery utilization reaches 93.5% capacity
Oil futures settled slightly higher Oct. 15 as latest US Energy Information Administration data showed an eight-millionbarrel draw in crude stocks in the week to Oct. 9, driven by growing demand.
NYMEX November WTI settled $2.88/barrel higher at $97.97/ barrel, and ICE December Brent rose 14 cents/b to $105.92/b. NYMEX November RBOB fell 7.93 cents/gallon to $3.1802/ gal, while November ULSD settled 8.18 cents/gal lower at $4.8456/gal.
Total commercial crude stocks were around 415.6 million barrels, a low not seen since July 31, when inventories were at 406.9 million barrels.
The stock draw was fueled on the US Gulf Coast, where inventories fell by 9.2 million barrels to 234.4 million barrels. This marks the lowest level in the region since July 31.
The decline is broadly supportive for crude futures, particularly given the 0.8% increase in refinery utilization to 93.5% of operable capacity. However, the bullish signal was tempered by a 1.8-million-barrel build at Cushing, Oklahoma, which lifted stocks to 26.6 million barrels.
Gasoline inventories climbed 3.5 million barrels to 208.3 million barrels, led by gains on the USGC where inventories climbed 3 million barrels to 78 million barrels over the same period.

International Crude
North Sea crude backwardation narrows on uncertainty, falling margins
Week 2/3 Brent CFD heard in contango
Paper volatility tracks physical uncertainty
The backwardation in the North Sea crude oil market extended declines in early Oct. 15 European trade, as uncertainty in physical markets and weakening downstream margins pressured prompt derivative contracts.
As of 0930 GMT Oct. 15, brokers pegged the Week 2 Brent CFD contract, currently Oct. 26-30, at a premium of between $2.55/b and $2.85/b over its Week 6 counterpart.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the corresponding time spread at $3.60/b at the Oct. 14 London close, and $9.50/b at the start of the week.
The prompt Week 2/Week 3 time spread was heard pegged at between minus 15 cents/b and minus 8 cents/b as of 1030 GMT.
For now, the contango was only seen in the Oct. 26-30 weekly roll with the rest of the curve still in backwardation, according to one broker.
"Today, [CFD structure] is dropping like a stone," a Europebased derivatives trader said Oct. 15. "There's massive uncertainty in the physical market ... the volatility on the crude side is massive."
However, price action in early European trade may reflect an oversold market, based on observed paper flows in the session so far, the trader added.
The North Sea crude oil complex has come under increasing pressure throughout the week as the punitive freight environment, which initially bolstered differentials of the region's short-haul cargoes, began to squeeze downstream margins.
-- Ernest Puey

Northwest European sour crude outlook constructive amid wide sulfur spread
Refineries eye sulfur switch amid margin squeeze
Johan Sverdrup demand expected to firm

European crude oil traders foresee strengthening differentials in the North Sea sour crude complex amid a wide sweet and sour crude spread.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 15, 2026

Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Dated Brent physical differential -- reflecting light sweet barrels in the North Sea -- at a premium of $16.81/b Oct. 15.
That's compared with the Johan Sverdrup FOB Mongstad physical differential of $5.685/b Oct. 15.
On a delivered basis to Rotterdam, two traders have estimated the spread between WTI Midland and Johan Sverdrup to be around $10/b.
For now, first-decade November loading Johan Sverdrup cargoes were last heard trading around a premium of $6/b over Dated Brent.
While North Sea sour barrels have benefitted from the same short-haul advantages as its sweet regional counterparts in the current freight environment, upward momentum in the prompt European sour market was hampered by competitively priced alternatives from the Middle East.
However, with light sweet crude barrels pricing at a hefty premium to sour volumes, market participants expect refineries to turn towards sour feedstock to maximize their margins.
"Johan Sverdrup should be a bit more popular on the back of [the wide sweet-sour spread]," a sour crude trader said Oct. 15, noting that Mediterranean sour barrels have strengthened. "All it will take is for an outage [from Middle East supply], and it's going to firm again," the trader said.
Several other Europe-based crude traders have acknowledged the potential for such a switch to occur, noting the cargo movements in the first half of the week.
"There're better margins if you can switch," another sour crude oil trader said, pointing to the spread on delivered cargoes in the North Sea.
Nevertheless, the economics of such a switch is a complex calculation that goes beyond the spot value of crude barrels alone.
For example, a slight retreat in transatlantic freight rates may complicate the sulfur economics for barrels in the North Sea region and narrow the sulfur arbitrage.
A third trader said there "hasn't been much evidence" of such a switch occurring yet, although it "would make sense for people to buy [sour grades]".
Platts assessed the US Gulf Coast-UK/continent Aframax freight rate at $182.79/metric ton Oct. 14, down $11.55/mt on the day. However, the figure still remains above its March 30 peak of $173.33/mt.
"Freight coming off changes the landed WTI economics, so I think sweet [grades] come off," the third trader said.
For now, traders are awaiting indicative levels for seconddecade loading Johan Sverdrup with few cargoes heard trading for that segment.
-- Ernest Puey
Middle East crude differentials hit 2-month low amid rise in spot sales
Platts cash Dubai differential down 40% in Oct to $7.35/b
Spot offers help to offset lower arbitrage arrivals

Middle East crude oil differentials have fallen to their lowest level in more than two months as a rise in spot sales by regional producers, combined with refinery run cuts across parts of Asia, has helped offset supply tightness, according to trade sources and Platts data over the week to Oct. 15.
The benchmark Platts cash Dubai differential against samemonth Dubai futures fell to a $7.35/b premium at the Oct. 14 Asian close, down more than 40% month-to-date and its lowest since early August, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
Saudi Aramco sold up to 20 VLCCs of crude cargoes for November lifting or delivery to end-users across Asia, including buyers in China, South Korea, India and Japan, in spot sales during the week ended Oct. 9, according to at least three Asia-based trade sources, including two who were directly involved in the trades. This was in addition to regular term commitments that included liftings from the Red Sea port of Yanbu and ship-to-ship transfers outside the Gulf, two term lifters of Saudi cargoes said.
Abu Dhabi National Oil Co. also sold close to 20 million barrels of its offshore grades Upper Zakum and Das Blend in its eleventh tender issued since the start of the war, with cargoes going to refiners in China, Taiwan, Japan and India, at least four Asiabased crude traders said.
Saudi Aramco declined to comment in an Oct. 15 email response to Platts, while ADNOC said it does not comment on commercial matters in an Oct. 15 email response.
QatarEnergy in the week beginning Oct. 12 also offered AlShaheen, Qatar Land and Qatar Marine for various November laycans, Platts reported Oct. 13.
Asian refinery sources reported sales of Iraqi Basrah crude in recent weeks, with around eight Chinese independent refiners purchasing parcels of Basrah Medium and Heavy since early September as they sought alternatives to disrupted Iranian crude supplies.
The recent spot sales and offers, accompanied by data showing ongoing successful tanker transits through the Strait of Hormuz, have helped limit tightness in Asia as surging freight rates impeded sales of arbitrage crudes to the region, two Singapore-based crude traders said.
Trade sources have also noted refinery run cuts in parts of Asia due to falling domestic margins, while refiners in the Chinese independent sector are also facing sharply reduced Iranian crude supply as the US continues to block Iranian oil exports.
-- Yong ren Toh, Alvinn Philips, Irene Tang
Gulf states accelerate pipeline projects to reduce Hormuz reliance
Diversification shifts risk, creates new chokepoints
Goldman sees capacity additions hitting 7.3 mil b/d by 2028
Qatar rejects pipelines, maintains Hormuz route
The Iran war is accelerating a redesign of Middle East oil export infrastructure as Gulf producers expand routes that

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

3

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 15, 2026

reduce tanker exposure to the Strait of Hormuz, but funding, political and security hurdles remain, according to analysts and company executives.
For decades, Gulf producers optimized around a single reality: most exports would move by tanker through the Strait of Hormuz. The US-Iran war has exposed the vulnerability of that model. Governments, national oil companies, traders and importers are now treating Hormuz disruption as a recurring strategic risk rather than a remote contingency. A key challenge, however, is that bypassing Hormuz reduces concentration risk, but it does not eliminate geopolitical risk, according to the analysts.
More than 20 million b/d of crude and refined products, roughly 20% of global trade, transited Hormuz before the war began in February 2026, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea. Although crude flows have recovered in recent weeks -after collapsing to below 5 million in August- total oil shipments through the chokepoint remain below pre-war levels, CAS data shows.
Faced with the largest oil supply disruption in history, the response has focused on new regional pipelines, ports and storage hubs.
Saudi Arabia has reverted to its East-West pipeline to shift crude to the Red Sea on its west coast, avoiding tanker exposure around the strait. The UAE has redirected barrels through its Habshan-Fujairah pipeline, which bypasses Hormuz entirely, and plans to double that capacity by next year. Iraq also trucks crude into Syria, with exports capable of reaching 200,000 b/d, according to an Aug. 19 report by S&P Global Energy CERA.

Iraq has focused on the 2.25 million b/d Basrah-Haditha trunk pipeline, intended to link southern production with three potential export corridors: Ceyhan in Turkey, Banias in Syria and Aqaba in Jordan, according to the CERA report. The Kirkuk-Banias project remains in the study and rehabilitation phase, it said.
TotalEnergies has told UAE state oil company Abu Dhabi National Oil Co. that it is "ready to participate" in doubling capacity to Fujairah, CEO Patrick Pouyanne told investors on Sept. 28. Saudi Arabia has also begun work on a pipeline to Duqm, Oman, he said. "There is this Baghdad Iraq-Syria pipeline in which we will participate as well," he said. Aramco declined to comment, and the UAE's energy ministry did not respond to requests for comment.
The rerouting is making Gulf-produced barrels more competitive for top oil consumers in Asia on the freight side. Platts assessed the traditional Persian Gulf-China VLCC rate at $255.72/metric ton Oct. 9, on top of additional war risk premiums exceeding 10% of hull value on the charterer's account. In comparison, VLCC rates for loading from the Gulf of Oman were $167.65/mt and from Yanbu at $214.08/mt for Far East destinations, and importers face much lower insurance premiums.
But the recent Houthi blockade on Saudi shipping has displayed how such a rerouting strategy could be further complicated by security risks. With the Yemen-based militants located in close proximity to the Bab al-Mandab Strait, Saudi oil at times needs to travel to Asia via Egypt's Suez Canal or the SUMED pipeline, Mediterranean and Cape of Good Hope, adding 29 days to voyage time to India and 25 days to China.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

Pipeline limits The scale of the proposed infrastructure build is significant.
Goldman Sachs estimated in a July report that effective pipeline capacity bypassing Hormuz, concentrated in Saudi Arabia, the UAE and Iraq, will rise by 3.8 million b/d by end-2027 and 7.3 million b/d cumulatively by end-2028.
"This implies over 14 million b/d of effective pipeline capacity by end-2028, which would insulate more than 60% of the 23 million b/d of pre-war oil exports by the seven Persian Gulf countries that geographically need to rely on pipelines to de-risk exports from Hormuz disruptions," the analysts said. Goldman's 23 million b/d covers oil exports from the seven Gulf states while CAS measures total Hormuz throughput including products and non-oil cargoes.
Workarounds in the Strait of Hormuz, such as an oil shuttle service to the Gulf of Oman involving ship-to-ship transfers, are expected to keep oil moving, with Middle East crude oil exports, including from Iran, below prewar levels through 2027 and 2028, S&P Global Energy CERA analysts said in a Sept. 29 report.
"Apart from oil already afloat, Iranian crude exports have dropped to zero, although some barrels may be leaking out through difficult-to-see channels," the analysts said.
Some of the proposed pipeline projects have been stalled for years, held up by prolonged discussions and financial or technical setbacks. Despite a new impetus to resolve Hormuz shipping risks, hurdles still remain in opening alternative routes.
"Early steps are visible in the UAE and Iraq, but funding, politics, and security remain binding constraints, exposing a deep vulnerability in Middle East oil logistics," oil analysts at S&P Global CERA said in an August note.
Pipelines also only fix part of the problem. "The pipelines only address crude oil, not, mostly, refined products, and not at all LNG, or the many other commodities and goods shipped in and out of the Strait of Hormuz," said Robin Mills, CEO of consultancy Qamar Energy, on Sept. 16. Alternative pipelines will take years to build, and countries such as Kuwait and Qatar lack "easy" alternatives, he said.
Qatar, the world's largest LNG exporter, has ruled out pipelines altogether. "We have decided not to use pipelines as an alternative based on commercial and technical criteria," QatarEnergy CEO Saad Kaabi, who is also Qatar's energy minister,

told the Qatar Economic Forum in New York on Sept. 20. "It is wrong to call the Strait of Hormuz obsolete as this
waterway carries trade in all products, not only oil and gas, to and from all its adjacent countries," Kaabi said.
Diversification, not replacement One paradox is that bypassing Hormuz does not eliminate
geopolitical risk; it redistributes it, potentially creating new chokepoints. As a result, some analysts frame the planned diversions as risk management rather than escape.
"I see these efforts to divert exports away from Hormuz as diversification rather than replacement," said Dong Wang, senior Middle East oil analyst at S&P Global CERA, on Sept. 16. "Reliance on Hormuz can be reduced, but Hormuz itself cannot be replaced."
Carole Nakhle, CEO of Crystol Energy and secretary general of the Arab Energy Club, said on Sept. 22 that new routes "reinforce the importance of diversity: more alternative routes, more supply from elsewhere and a more diversified energy mix."
The open question, she said, is whether a Hormuz disruption will still carry the same market impact in two years. "If alternative export routes are expanded and more oil and gas supply comes from other regions, that impact may well diminish. Diversification reduces concentration risk; it does not eliminate risk altogether."
With crude exports diverting from Hormuz, risks move elsewhere, including the Bab al-Mandab Strait into the Red Sea and Fujairah, UAE. Saudi Arabia's switch from exporting from its east coast to the west coast swapped Houthi risk for Hormuz risk. Only time will tell if traffic snaps back to Hormuz when the war ends or if the new map is here to stay. -- Claudia Carpenter, Lauren Holtmeier, Lulwa Albuainain, Max Lin,
Faleh Al-Khayat

Asia-Oceania ultra-light crudes likely to sustain premiums as Middle East supply stays tight
Ichthys premiums averaged $16.32/b in October Thailand takes Alba condensate amid high Australian
premiums Seoul seeks to cut costs on Malaysian, Australian

scheduled to load Oct. 28-Nov. 1 at a premium equivalent to around $8/barrel over Platts Dated Brent, FOB.
Japan has made occasional purchases of LSC spot cargoes, but has struggled to secure even a single cargo so far this year.
Meanwhile, Japan saw its crude and condensate imports from Qatar tumble 84% year over year to 3.9 million barrels in the first eight months, the latest data from the Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry issued Sept. 30 showed. Japanese refiners have received some medium-sour Qatar Marine crude so far this year, but no shipments of DFC or LSC, METI data showed.
However, Japan has been bringing home some of its equity ultra-light crude barrels from Australia, with 626,000 barrels of Ichthys condensate discharging back in August, METI data issued Sept. 30 showed.
Most recently, Japan's Mitsubishi was heard to have moved its equity Australian North West Shelf condensate cargo for loading over Oct. 20-24.
Reflecting East Asia's heavy reliance on Australian ultralight crudes, Platts assessed Ichthys condensate at an average premium of $16.32/b to Dated Brent, FOB to date in October, compared to the September average premium of $11.87 and the 2025 average of $3.01/b.

Southeast Asian and Oceania ultra-light crude cargoes are expected to maintain spot-market premiums amid ongoing uncertainty in Middle Eastern deodorized field and low-sulfur condensate supplies, as South Korea streamlines customs procedures and revises tariffs to lower feedstock import costs, industry sources and Korea Customs Service said.
Refiners and petrochemical producers with condensate splitters for naphtha output are heavily relying on Australian, Malaysian and African ultra-light crudes, after struggling to secure steady Middle East DFC and LSC supplies since the Middle East war started in March, feedstock managers at Japanese, South Korean, Chinese and Thai refiners in Ibaraki, Incheon, Seosan, Fujian and Rayong, as well as Singaporebased traders, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, in market discussions Oct. 9-15.
Among recent spot deals, a South Korean refiner purchased a 600,000-barrel cargo of Australian Ichthys Field condensate

Platts also assessed North West Shelf condensate at an average premium of $3.33/b to Dated Brent, FOB to date in October, compared to the September average premium of $2.24 and the 2025 average of minus $2.12/b.

With Australian condensate premiums elevated, some downstream players are looking further afield for ultra-light crude supply. Feedstock and logistics managers at Thai and Chinese refining and petrochemical companies in Rayong and Fujian told Platts that African grades are emerging as an alternative.
Thailand imported 14,818 b/d of Alen and Alba condensate from Equatorial Guinea in August, compared with zero shipments from the African supplier in the previous month and the same month a year earlier, according to the latest Thai customs data.
In China, shipments of ultra-light crude and natural gas liquids from Russia's Sakhalin 2 project to Dongshan Bay of Gulei in Fujian province surged 133% year over year to 9.75 million barrels in the first nine months of the year, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
Sakhalin 2 is an oil, gas and condensate project located in the Sea of Okhotsk offshore Russia's Far East.
Seoul's customs, tax support Amid the tightening supply and rising premiums, Korea
Customs Service is taking steps to lower feedstock procurement costs for domestic refiners and petrochemical producers. KCS said in a statement issued Oct. 12 and its October industry briefing report that it is expanding customs support to facilitate imports of Malaysian condensate and Australian natural gas liquids.
Delayed certificates of origin hindered Malaysian condensate importers from securing preferential tariffs under the MalaysiaSouth Korea FTA, but after consultations with Malaysian authorities, oil companies, producers and exporters, the issuance period has been cut from an average of six months post-export to under two months, KCS said.
Among several Malaysian spot cargo deals concluded in recent trading cycles, a major South Korean refiner based in Incheon had purchased Cakerawala condensate loaded over Sept. 5-11 at a premium of around $5/b to Platts Dated Brent, FOB.
KCS also said it used its fast-track advance tariff classification process in May to reclassify Australian NGLs, a naphtha substitute, as petroleum products rather than crude oil, reducing the applicable tariff to 0% from 3% and removing the stockpiling obligation. The change allows petrochemical companies without

dedicated storage facilities to import the material more easily. "The measure could support annual imports of up to 2.5
million tonnes," the South Korean customs department said. -- Gawoon Philip Vahn, Leon Wong and Daisy Xu - Oil Market Specialists
IEA accelerates oil stock release, plans over 20 mil barrels of diesel
About 330 mil barrels released so far
Total draw to exceed original 400 mil-barrel pledge
Members urged to raise crude, refined-product supply
International Energy Agency members will accelerate the release of emergency oil stocks, including more than 20 million barrels of diesel, as tight supplies continue to strain global fuel markets.
The IEA Governing Board said Oct. 15 that about 330 million barrels had been released under its March 2026 Collective Action, and members would take steps to complete the remaining releases of approximately 100 million barrels.
Some members have released more barrels than initially pledged, while others intend to do so, meaning the ultimate draw will exceed the original 400 million-barrel commitment, the agency said.
Specific plans are already in place to release more than 20 million barrels of diesel "as soon as possible," according to the IEA. The agency did not detail the timing or geographical distribution of the diesel release.
The IEA also encouraged members with oil production and refining capacity to increase supplies of crude and refined products, particularly diesel, while stressing the importance of maintaining the free flow of energy trade.
The decision follows an Oct. 2 agreement under which G7 leaders set a four-month deadline for releasing the outstanding 100 million barrels pledged in March, as previously reported by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy. The G7 called for a substantial diesel release to be frontloaded within 20 days and pledged to avoid energy export restrictions among members.
The IEA's March collective action, launched in response to the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz, was the largest coordinated stock release in its history. Deliveries had reached 300 million barrels by August, including 110 million barrels from the Americas, 112 million barrels from Asia-Oceania, and 68 million barrels from Europe, Platts previously reported.
Out of 331 million barrels released, about 240 million barrels were state inventories, while 91 million had been released from obligated industry stocks, according to the latest IEA Oil Market report.
Only 8 million barrels were released in September, marking a significant decline in emergency stock releases in recent months, according to the report.
Platts assessed the benchmark European diesel price, ULSD 10ppmS CIF NWE Cargo, at $1,471.75/metric ton Oct.15, down from

Equatorial Guinea plans Zafiro field output boost, major gas expansion
Zafiro field could hit 50,000 b/d with partner
Eyes 10 gas fields to feed key EG LNG plant
Issues new laws to lure upstream investors
Equatorial Guinea is hoping to hit 30,000 b/d at its flagship Zafiro oil field - which ExxonMobil exited in 2024 - through an upcoming well reconnection project, but could reach 50,000 b/d by bringing a partner on board, the head of state-run GEPetrol said Oct. 15.
In a press conference on the sidelines of the Africa Energy Week conference in Cape Town, Bienvenido Nguema Envo, director general of the African country's national oil company, said 7,000-11,000 barrels/day of crude output could be added in 2027 by reconnecting old wells.
"Together with the workovers they present an initial phase to ramp up production to just north of 30,000 b/d for Zafiro," Envo said.
The country has exported just 14,000 b/d of medium sweet Zafiro crude so far this year, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
Envo added: "There is a much bigger project, the redevelopment of block B and the Zafiro field. There are enough studies right now to justify the project. So we are looking for some partners to be able to execute that."
"Numbers vary on how far we can take the production, but certainly north of 50,000 b/d" he said.
That will require new wells and an analysis of whether the existing facilities on the field can handle additional volumes, Envo said.
The Zafiro field has historically been the cornerstone of Equatorial Guinea's oil sector, but was taken into state hands in the second quarter of 2024 when ExxonMobil exited the country. It pumped some 280,000 b/d at peak in 2004 but has faced major natural declines.
GEPetrol has limited experience operating assets, especially of Zafiro's size. Envo did not specify any potential partners for the development.
At the same press conference, hydrocarbons minister Antonio Oburu Ondo said that the small OPEC member was working hard to attract new upstream investment, by "improving our legal and regulatory competitiveness" to be "absolutely competitive and to modernize our terms."
The country is introducing a new hydrocarbons law this month, and a special economic zones law, to attract new investors, he said.
Equatorial Guinea once pumped above 300,000 b/d but

currently produces some 50,000 b/d, according to official figures, amid underinvestment and field maturation.
Gas plans On the gas side, Ondo said the country was assessing key
gas-rich blocks, specifically EG-31, Block 11, Block 5, Block 27 and Block B4, to extend the commercial life of its 3.7 million metric ton/year EG LNG facility.
It follows Chevron's recent final investment decision on the large Aseng gas project, which is expected to see first gas in 2028.
The country's EG LNG plant could also process gas from neighboring Cameroon and Nigeria, Ondo said. "As we see all these gas resources coming to EG LNG at some point we have to talk about extending the capacity or going to a second train," he added.
With ambitions to create a "gas mega hub" for the Central African region, Equatorial Guinea could ultimately produce gas from as many as 10 fields simultaneously along the so-called West Belt, Ondo said, and process it at the EG LNG plant.
One key focus is Fortuna FLNG, a delayed project on Block EG27 that is the subject of talks between Equatorial Guinea and US company ConocoPhillips. The field was discovered in 2008.
Ondo said Italy's Eni was also interested in the project, as well as "a couple of [other] companies." He said that the government still needs "to strike an outcome in our negotiation with ConocoPhillips."
"EG-27 is the biggest target here - that is very important," Ondo said of the plans for the next year. "We have to be able to say that EG-27, we have at least a timeline for the FID."
The minister added that he had met his Senegalese counterpart in Cape Town and had signed a cooperation agreement on energy and mining between the two countries.
-- Charlie Mitchell
Indonesia's state auditor questions basis for 1 million b/d oil target
BPK cites export policy gaps
Finds oil output goal lacks supporting data
Enhanced recovery projects may be insufficient
Indonesia's state auditor has questioned the basis for the country's goal of raising oil and condensate production to 1 million b/d by 2030, saying key assumptions underpinning the target were not supported by valid data and warning that exploration and enhanced oil recovery programs may be insufficient to deliver the planned increase, according to its audit summary seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Oct. 14.
The findings come after the Audit Board of the Republic of Indonesia (BPK) reviewed upstream oil and gas management and commercialization policies. The audit also found shortcomings in the government's efforts to redirect more domestic crude and condensate to local refineries.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

9

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 15, 2026

According to the audit summary, BPK found that estimates of the contribution from exploration activities to future oil and gas lifting were inaccurate. The auditor also said the process of translating state budget production targets into contractorlevel targets did not use up-to-date data on production-sharing contractors' technical capabilities.
BPK further said the consolidated long-term plan for 2030 contained lifting targets it considered invalid and that had not been agreed with production-sharing contractors. Similar concerns were raised regarding assumptions underpinning Indonesia's target of producing 12 Bcf/d of gas by 2030.
Indonesia's average oil production stood at 578,156 b/d as of July 31, below the government's 2026 target of 610,000 b/d, according to the audit summary. Indonesia's crude and condensate lifting target is set at 602,000-615,000 b/d in 2027. Gas lifting is projected at 9.34-9.77 Bcf/d in 2027, versus 9.84 Bcf/d this year.
BPK also said enhanced oil recovery projects had yet to deliver significant results due to constraints related to facilities, project economics, and delays in subsurface studies. The auditor said these limitations could affect achievement of the government's broader energy-planning targets of 1 million b/d of oil production and 12 Bcf/d of gas production by 2030.
Domestic supply policy BPK also said Indonesia's policy of prioritizing domestic crude
and condensate supplies was not fully implemented. The Energy and Mineral Resources Ministry introduced the
policy in January, 2025 to strengthen domestic energy security by redirecting crude oil and condensate supplies to domestic refineries. The policy covered the government's share of crude oil, crude oil belonging to production-sharing contractors, and the government's share of condensate.
However, BPK said that the upstream regulator, SKK Migas, redirected only the government's share of oil to the domestic market. SKK Migas and production-sharing contractors did not revise export plans for 14 crude oil and condensate grades produced by contractors, resulting in exports of 15.38 million barrels of crude oil and condensate worth about $1.03 billion in 2025, BPK said.
According to BPK, the exports reduced the volume available for domestic supply by the same amount. The auditor recommended that SKK Migas review implementation of the commercialization policy and improve coordination with the ministry, state-owned Pertamina, refinery operators, and contractors to better support domestic supply priorities.
-- Mia Pei, Anita Nugraha
CHINA DATA: Independent Russian crude imports hit 6-month high; Iranian flows fall
Jan-Sep Russian crude imports rise 71%
Russian gains fail to offset declines in Iranian supply
ESPO premiums drop $3/b

China's independent refiners increased imports of Russian crude in September as tighter Iranian supplies drove a shift in feedstock, with volumes rising 26.2% month over month to a sixmonth high of 5.4 million metric tons, or 1.32 million barrels/day, according to data collected by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, from industry sources Oct. 15.
However, higher Russian inflows were insufficient to offset the decline in Iranian crude imports, which fell 57.3% month over month to 2.2 million mt, reaching their lowest level since January 2022, when deliveries were 2.18 million mt, the data showed.
Combined imports of Russian and Iranian crude by China's independent refining sector fell 22% month over month to 7.59 million mt in September, according to the data.
China's independent refiners imported 3.4 million mt of ESPO crude in September, equivalent to 34 cargoes of 100,000 mt each, up from 3.2 million mt in August, the data showed.
A 400,000 b/d private refinery in Shandong was among the largest ESPO buyers, purchasing at least six cargoes during the month, according to refinery sources.
Imports of other Russian grades also increased. Combined arrivals of Sokol and Urals crude reached 1.4 million mt in September, up 23% from August, the data showed. Over January-September, China's independent refiners imported 43.22 million mt of Russian crude, up 70.8% year over year, according to the data.
ESPO premiums fall Mainstream offers for December-delivery ESPO crude were
reported at premiums of about $25/b to ICE Brent futures on a DES Shandong basis on Oct. 15, down from about $28/b in the week ended Oct. 9, refinery sources said. No deals were concluded at the current level.
"With prices for alternative crudes falling, independent refiners are waiting for ESPO prices to come down," a Dongyingbased refinery source said.
The refinery source said ESPO cargoes are unlikely to clear at current premiums, increasing the likelihood of further price declines.
"Deals for December cargoes are difficult to conclude because prices remain too high for buyers," a Shandong-based trader said.
Market participants indicated that offers for December ESPO had fallen to around $20/b as of Oct. 15, with some independent refiners targeting prices as low as $16/b or even $13/b, according to two refinery sources in Shandong.
"Those levels are too low to be reachable at the moment," a refinery source in Dongying said, referring to the $13/b-$16/b range.
November-loading ESPO cargoes were reported to be traded at premiums of about $30/b to ICE Brent futures on Oct. 15, according to refinery sources. Premiums were about $12/b at the beginning of September.
-- Daisy Xu - Oil Market Specialist, Oceana Zhou - Oil Market Specialist

JAPAN DATA: Refinery runs rise to 79% over Oct. 4-10 on higher crude throughput
Crude throughput rises 3.9% WOW to 2.45 mil b/d
Three refining units shut in Japan as of Oct. 10
Idemitsu restarts Keihin CDU after prolonged maintenance
Japan's refinery run rates rose to 78.8% over Oct. 4-10, from 75.9% the previous week, amid higher crude throughput, data from the Petroleum Association of Japan showed Oct. 15.
The country's crude throughput increased 3.9% week over week to 2.45 million barrels/day, the data showed.
As of Oct. 10, three crude distillation units were offline across Japan, according to calculations by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
Japan's second-largest refiner, Idemitsu Kosan, restarted the sole CDU at its Keihin refinery in Tokyo Bay on Oct. 7 after completing scheduled maintenance.
Idemitsu suspended operations at the Keihin CDU on March 22, initially planning to resume in July, a company spokesperson said, but did not mention the reason for the prolonged maintenance.
The nameplate capacity of the Keihin CDU stood at 70,000 b/d as of March 22, when operations were suspended. However, on April 1, Idemitsu lowered the capacity to 60,000 b/d following the termination of its business tie-up with Cosmo Oil.
Idemitsu shut one of the CDUs at its 225,000 b/d Yokkaichi refinery in central Japan for planned maintenance on Sept. 28.
The spokesperson did not disclose whether the No. 2 or No. 3 CDU had been shut, but a company official previously said the No. 2 CDU was scheduled for maintenance from end-September to mid-November.
Idemitsu reduced the nameplate capacity of its Yokkaichi refinery to 225,000 b/d from 255,000 b/d on April 1 but did not disclose the capacity breakdown for the No. 2 and No. 3 CDUs.
Meanwhile, ENEOS, Japan's largest refiner, shut the 95,200 b/d No. 2 CDU at its 200,200 b/d Mizushima-B plant in western Japan on Aug. 31, and the sole 153,000 b/d CDU at its Negishi refinery in Tokyo Bay on Sept. 7, both for planned maintenance.
Japan's crude stocks fell 0.3% week over week but rose 3.4% year over year to 66.94 million barrels in the week to Oct. 10, PAJ and Platts data showed Oct. 15. Unfinished oil product stocks totaled 42.79 million barrels, up 1% week over week and 3.8% year over year, the data showed.
-- Hiroyuki Koshoji

lowest level since November 2019, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea, as Iranian barrels accumulated closer to the Gulf region amid a US naval blockade and tighter sanctions pressure.
Total floating storage stocks in Southeast Asia fell 51.2% month over month to 11.24 million barrels end-September from 23.01 million barrels end-August, according to CAS data compiled Oct. 12. The volume was also down from 35.97 million barrels July 14, when the US reinstated its blockade of Iranian ports, and 49.19 million barrels April 13, when Washington imposed its first blockade.
Iranian crude and condensate accounted for most of the remaining Southeast Asian inventory, but those volumes also declined to 9.25 million barrels end-September from 17.8 million barrels a month earlier, CAS data showed.
Iranian floating storage has now fallen about 65% since July 14 and nearly 78% since April 13, when it stood at 26.76 million barrels and 41.45 million barrels, respectively, the data showed.
According to CAS, a ship is considered floating storage if it is laden and does not move more than 10 nautical miles per day for at least seven consecutive days.
The data showed no qualifying floating storage record in Far Eastern waters at the end of September, compared with 4.62 million barrels of total floating storage, including 524,400 barrels of Iranian crude and condensate, at the end of August.
Barrels accumulate closer to Iran The sharp decline in Asian waters contrasts with a renewed
buildup in the Gulf. Total crude and condensate floating storage in Gulf waters
rose to 86.6 million barrels end-September from 69.83 million barrels a month earlier. Iranian crude and condensate increased 17.1% to 61.64 million barrels from 52.63 million barrels, CAS data showed.
Iranian Gulf storage was nearly three times the 20.71 million barrels recorded July 14 and more than six times the 9.51 million barrels held there immediately before the first US blockade in April, which restricted vessels entering or leaving Iranian ports.
The shift is even more pronounced than it was a year earlier. At the end of September 2025, Southeast Asian waters held 39.98 million barrels of floating crude and condensate, compared

For Iranian barrels specifically, Southeast Asia held 31.92 million barrels end-September 2025 compared with 10.01 million barrels in the Gulf. A year later, Southeast Asian Iranian storage had fallen to 9.25 million barrels while Gulf inventories had climbed to 61.64 million barrels, the data showed.
Washington's blockade has constrained the movement of fresh Iranian barrels out of the Gulf even as previously positioned cargoes in Southeast Asian waters have discharged or otherwise left floating storage. The US Central Command said Oct. 10 its forces had destroyed 10 Iran-linked tankers while turning around 135 ships during three months of blockade enforcement.
Benjamin Tan, director of liquid bulk research at CAS, noted the limited Iran-linked vessel transits amid the reinstated blockade: "Overall shipping traffic has held up despite the reinstated US naval blockade, increasing from 56 transits per day in September to 62 in October to date. The bigger impact is on Iran-linked shipping, where detected non-compliant traffic remains at just three vessels per day and is dominated by bulkers, LPG carriers and smaller tankers rather than crude tankers."
China flows key to drawdown China's independent refiners imported 2.197 million mt of
Iranian crude in September, or 537,000 barrels/day, down 59% month over month and 58% year over year, hitting the lowest level since January 2022, according to data compiled by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
The decrease in import levels coincided with a drop in Iranian crude availability, driving Iranian Light offers to about $25/b above ICE Brent on a DES Shandong basis, putting the grade closer to the premium commanded by Russia's ESPO crude, according to Shandong-based market participants Oct. 13.
Iranian oil in floating storage Despite the decline in Southeast Asian inventories, the overall
volume of Iranian crude and condensate held in floating storage across the tracked Southeast Asian, Far Eastern and Middle East

Gulf waters was virtually unchanged month over month, the CAS data showed.
Combined Iranian floating storage in the regions stood at 70.89 million barrels end-September, compared with 70.95 million barrels end-August. However, the figure was 43.1% above the 49.52 million barrels recorded on July 14, 37.3% above the April 13 level and 46.6% higher year over year, CAS said.
-- Staff
Platts Asia Pacific Sweet Crude Daily Market Analysis
Dec-loading Ichthys Field condensate moves
Pertamina seeks various crude grades for Jan arrival
Asia-Oceania ultra-light crudes may sustain premiums on tight Middle East supply
The Asia-Pacific regional complex saw movements across the condensate segment on Oct. 15, along with tender issuances for January-arrival crude barrels.
In the regional condensate complex, initial trade details surfaced as France's TotalEnergies was heard to have sold its Australian Ichthys Field condensate cargo scheduled to load over Dec. 11-15, with initial trade levels heard in the high singles to low teens against Dated Brent, FOB, several trade sources said.
A Middle Eastern refiner was heard to have offered its deodorized field condensate cargo scheduled for November loading from inside the Strait of Hormuz, via a tender that closed Oct. 15.
Indonesia's Pertamina was seen seeking various crude grades for January arrival via a tender that closes Oct. 19, with validity until Oct. 21. The refiner previously sought 950,000 barrels of various crude grades scheduled for Dec. 17-19 arrival at its Balikpapan refinery via a tender that closed Oct. 13. The tender results could not be confirmed.
In the arbitrage crude complex, a US refiner was heard to have purchased Brazilian crude scheduled for end-December arrival at premiums in the high $10s/b against ICE Brent, DES USWC, though further details could not be confirmed.
Southeast Asian and Oceania ultra-light crude cargoes are expected to maintain spot-market premiums amid ongoing uncertainty in Middle Eastern deodorized field and low-sulfur condensate supplies, as South Korea streamlines customs procedures and revises tariffs to lower feedstock import costs, industry sources and Korea Customs Service said.
Refiners and petrochemical producers with condensate splitters for naphtha output are heavily relying on Australian, Malaysian and African ultra-light crudes, after struggling to secure steady Middle East DFC and LSC supplies since the Middle East war started in March, feedstock managers at Japanese, South Korean, Chinese and Thai refiners in Ibaraki, Incheon, Seosan, Fujian and Rayong, as well as Singaporebased traders, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, in market discussions Oct. 9-15.

Amid the tightening supply and rising premiums, Korea Customs Service is taking steps to lower feedstock procurement costs for domestic refiners and petrochemical producers. KCS said in a statement issued Oct. 12 and its October industry briefing report that it is expanding customs support to facilitate imports of Malaysian condensate and Australian natural gas liquids.
Delayed certificates of origin hindered Malaysian condensate importers from securing preferential tariffs under the MalaysiaSouth Korea FTA, but after consultations with Malaysian authorities, oil companies, producers and exporters, the issuance period has been cut from an average of six months post-export to under two months, KCS said.

Platts Mediterranean & Black Sea Sweet Crude Daily Commentary
Three Azeri Light trades in MOC
Med sweets correct as margins erode
The Mediterranean sweet complex corrected Oct. 15 after three cargoes of Azeri Light traded in the Platts Market on Close assessment process, bringing the differential down by over $5/ barrel day over day.
Azeri Light strengthened earlier in October amid high diesel cracks and expensive freight, pushing the Azeri grade to all-time highs. But after the November loading program was released at the end of last week, trading activity picked up, with market participants noting that lower product prices and elevated freight were eroding refining margins, thereby diminishing buying appetite even for the diesel-rich Azeri.
A day after the MOC saw three outstanding offers at the close Oct. 14, the sellers returned with their offers, starting at a $15.70/b premium to Dated Brent for BP PLC's cargo delivered Nov. 10-14. ITOCHU Corp. soon joined, offering a Nov. 4-8 cargo at a $15.70/b premium.
By the time SOCAR Inc. joined the offer side around 1530 London time, the prices of the two offers were below $13/b, down $3/b from when they were floated around 1430.
"Freight is coming off and the rise in Azeri prices was partly due to the freight," a Mediterranean-based source said. "The refining margins also thin and so I am not surprised by the fall in the differential."
OMV AG lifted SOCAR's offer at a $11.45/b premium, while BP and Itochu had their offers lifted at $10.20/b and $10.40/b, respectively, just before the MOC close. SOCAR reoffered a cargo, and it remained outstanding at the end of the pricing session at $10.55/b premium to Dated Brent.
Azeri Light differential averaged $16.57/b Oct. 1-14, Platts data showed.
"Azeri is being offered down fast," a second Med-based source said during the MOC. "It will bring the whole complex with it; other

grades have to follow." The correction in Azeri differentials came as other regional
grades continued to soften, particularly Saharan Blend and CPC Blend, which often rely on arbitrage to clear monthly volumes.
"High prices that we saw last week eroded refining margins and the market turned long with all the supply available," a third Med source said.
-- Jan Kedzior
Oil NWE and Med Weekly Sour Crude Market Analysis
Johan Sverdrup differentials fall
Iraqi crude keeps Med well-supplied
Freight could complicate November trading
European sour grades weakened on the week with the downward trend most pronounced in the North Sea where Johan Sverdrup FOB differentials have fallen by around $10/b over the week.
Values for October-loading prompt cargoes have come off in recent sessions with alternative availabilities from the Middle East, including Iraqi Basrah Medium crude, landing competitively into the region.
"Johan Sverdrup has suffered downward pressure as [Iraqi] Basrah Medium arrival offers were cheap, so the spread between heavy grades and lighter crudes such as the Dated Brent basket grades seems quite high," a Europe-based crude trader said.
Values for November-arrivals of Basrah Medium delivered into the Mediterranean were reported by traders at premiums of between 50 cents/b to $2/b over November Dated Brent earlier in the week and fell November Dated Brent flat Oct. 9.
"Sours are going down, Johan Sverdrup needs to soften even more to compete with Basrah," said a second trader.
A steep backwardation between the Brent CFDs settling across October and November has also contributed to demonstrated pressure on prompt October-loading cargoes, said traders.
In the Mediterranean, market sources said there is still good availability of Kirkuk crude, keeping the region well supplied on top of the steady stream of Basrah Medium.
"Kirkuk, along with Basrah Medium, is keeping the sours low at the moment," a Med trader confirmed. "The market is long at the moment, and we see prices coming off with cargoes still available."
With Iraqi flows to the Med rising, market participants expect the sour complex to remain under pressure as the global balance absorbs increased supply from other countries.
"The US sour barrels [from the SPR release] and increased exports from Saudi Arabia will keep the sour complex wellsupplied and might even impact prices of diesel-rich crudes like Azeri Light," another Med source said.
But not all market participants anticipate a bearish November cycle for European sour crudes.

Surging freight rates across the Atlantic basin have complicated the supply picture ahead, possibly preventing SPR barrels from reaching Europe.
"The US SPR tender is ongoing still but I think given it's sour and freight is prohibitively expensive, it's likely to be unawarded or to stay domestic," said a North Sea third trader. "[However], the SPR barrels are all 620,000 to 680,000-barrel parcels on Aframaxes so if it does get exported at all, it will be more for Europe."
While SPR flows could be curtailed, traders were expecting more Latin American flows in the cycle ahead, with producers across the Americas expected to prioritize Europe as an export destination to save on longer-haul freight costs to Asia.
This could result in lengthening balances, but with competing barrels landing at elevated premiums -- potentially providing a floor to Johan Sverdrup differentials.

Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
Brent-related derivatives see volatile session
Freight rally stalls; drags on physical differentials
North Sea crude oil differentials softened further Oct. 15 in tandem with weaker backwardation and falling freight costs.
Backwardation along the North Sea Dated Brent strip saw a volatile session.
Prompt time spreads were heard trading in a contango earlier in the day, although paper flows suggested that the market was oversold, according to a Europe-based derivatives trader.
Platts assessed the Week 2 Brent CFD contract, currently Oct. 26-30, at a $3.15/barrel premium over its Week 6 counterpart, up about $1.30/b from midday pegs but still down 45 cents/b from the previous session.
"Today, [CFD structure] is dropping like a stone," the derivatives trader said Oct. 15. "There's massive uncertainty in the physical market ... the volatility on the crude side is massive."
During the Platts Market on Close assessment process, Glencore offered a WTI Midland cargo arriving in Rotterdam over Nov. 15-19. The cargo was lifted by Trafigura at a premium of $20.45/b over Dated Brent, reflecting weaker value on the day.
The decline in landed WTI Midland differentials tracked declining transatlantic freight costs in recent sessions.
Platts assessed the US Gulf Coast-UK/continent Aframax freight rate at $182.79/metric ton Oct. 14, down $11.55/mt on the day. However, the figure remained above its March 30 peak of $173.33/mt.
End-November arrivals of WTI Midland cargoes are anticipated to be heavy, with Asia pulling a smaller volume of the US grade due to hefty freight costs.
"Freight is too expensive. [There's] very little pull to Asia, so [volumes] will point at Europe," a North Sea crude oil trader said. "November could be a very different story from October."
Asian demand for feedstock cargoes remained satiated by

export volumes from the Persian Gulf, with outflows from the region recovering from disruptions brought about by the conflict in the Middle East.
However, even if the flows were to see fresh disruptions, two North Sea traders said they did not anticipate Asian buyers to lift WTI Midland volumes as replacement barrels, given the elevated freight costs and the ongoing squeeze on downstream margins.
"It might be difficult to get another rush for oil [in Asia]," another North Sea crude trader said, noting the impact of the elevated price environment on downstream demand.
With light sweet crude barrels in the North Sea still pricing at a healthy premium over its sour counterparts, traders expected refineries to turn toward more sulfurous barrels to lower feedstock costs.
While it appeared that refineries may not have yet implemented such a switch from sweet to sour crude, Europebased crude traders have said that sour barrels such as Johan Sverdrup are becoming more attractive to buyers.
For now, traders are awaiting indicative levels for seconddecade loading Johan Sverdrup with few cargoes heard trading at this point of the cycle.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Ernest Puey
Platts West African Crude Daily Commentary
Medium-heavy WAF segment heard still under pressure
Signs of November trading in Nigerian market emerge
There remained general bearishness in the West African crude market on Oct. 15, mainly due to persistently high freight costs, with plenty of November-loading cargoes still estimated by market participants to be unsold.
A major theme among market sources was current freeon-board differentials for medium and heavy crudes, including Angolan grades and Chad's Doba crude, many of which have seen values reach all-time lows in recent weeks, according to Platts assessments.
Views among players in the market differed on how much lower, if at all, prices would need to go given current dynamics in the Atlantic basin.
"It's hard to say if things have reached a floor. I still think we need barrels to arbitrage to Asia to really call it a floor," one WAF trader told Platts on the day. "If they just clear into Europe, where everything else is also pointing, then it becomes a race to the bottom."
On the other hand, another market participant said that given current freight costs, which had stabilized in recent days but remained high, free-on-board differentials would be expected to remain largely at similar values.
It was also said by a third trader that there had been signs of movement in the Nigerian market in the past few days, which had been heard quieter in recent weeks. Cargoes of grades including Utapate, Agbami and Escravos were reportedly sold, as were

-- Riley Tran

Producers restore over 90% of Gulf of Mexico shut-in oil volumes
Mars crude premium surges over $3/barrel
Four platforms keep workers evacuated still

Producers have restarted over 90% of the total crude production, with some 9.92% or 202,371 barrels/day of crude oil and 237 million metric cubic feet/d of natural gas remaining curtailed in the Gulf of Mexico in response to Hurricane Isaias, the Marine Minerals Administration said.
Personnel from four platforms remain evacuated, it said in its last update on Oct. 14, adding that the MMA team will work with offshore operators and other state and federal agencies until operations return to normal.
Producers, including Shell PLC, Chevron Corp., and BP PLC, have been proactively shutting in production and evacuating personnel from their platforms since Oct. 6, with curtailed crude oil and natural gas volumes totaling 185,120 b/d and 72 MMcf/d, MMA said at the time.
At its last count on Oct. 9, it said a total of 1.459 million b/d of oil and 1.26 billion cubic feet/d of natural gas production was shut in response to the hurricane.
Meanwhile, the differential for Mars on the US Gulf Coast had firmed up as producers cut production ahead of Hurricane Isaias.
Mars was assessed as strong as a $2.85/b premium to cash WTI Oct. 8, according to a Platts assessment. This was more than a $3/b increase from the Oct. 6 assessment, with a 50-cent/b discount to WTI.
Mars differentials have weakened as production returned. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, last assessed Mars at a $1.25/b premium over WTI Oct. 14.
Fellow sour grade Southern Green Canyon experienced a similar price move. Platts assessed SGC as strong as a $1.15/b discount to WTI Oct. 8. This was more than a $4/b increase from the $4.50/b discount to WTI assessed on Oct 6.
Platts last assessed SGC at a $5/b discount to cash WTI. -- Ashok Dutta, Alexander Saucer

US DATA: East Coast gasoline stocks fall as import decline outpaces refinery gains
Imports drop 52,000 b/d, pull stocks lower
Refinery rates climb 3.2 points to 81.1%
Inventories fall 186,000 barrels on week
East Coast gasoline inventories ticked lower during the week ended Oct. 9, as a pullback in waterborne imports offset higher domestic refinery utilization and output, according to Energy Information Administration data released Oct. 15.
Total gasoline stocks fell by 186,000 barrels week over week to 53.557 million barrels. The modest drawdown widened the Atlantic Coast's annual supply deficit, leaving regional inventories nearly 2.981 million barrels below levels recorded during the same period last year.

A decline in foreign arrivals contributed to the inventory draw. Gasoline imports into the East Coast dropped by 52,000 b/d week over week to average 370,000 b/d, slipping back below the 400,000 b/d mark after holding above 410,000 b/d over the previous two weeks. On an annual basis, import volumes trailed year-ago levels by 13,000 b/d.
The drop in foreign supply came as domestic refining activity picked up. East Coast refinery run rates rebounded 3.2 percentage points on the week to 81.1%, recovering after tumbling below 78% in early October. Despite the weekly improvement, operational throughput remained heavily depressed compared to last year's pace, sitting 12.3 percentage points lower year over year amid ongoing seasonal maintenance.
Refinery output tracked the higher utilization, with finished gasoline production increasing by 26,000 b/d week over week to average 3.146 million b/d. However, local production remained 72,000 b/d below the same week in 2025.
Overall, the data shows that while Atlantic Coast refiners lifted run rates back above 80%, the 26,000 b/d production gain was unable to bridge the gap left by the 52,000 b/d drop in foreign cargoes, resulting in a slight net draw on regional stockpiles.

US DATA: West Coast gasoline stocks fall despite surge in imports
Stocks fall 49,000 barrels to 27.8 mil total
Imports surge 44,000 b/d to 96,000 average
Refinery use drops 2 points to 93.2% rate
US West Coast gasoline inventories edged lower for a second consecutive week during the period ended Oct. 9, as steady regional demand kept stockpiles under pressure despite an import surge and an uptick in finished production, Energy Information Administration data showed Oct. 15.
Total gasoline stocks slipped by 49,000 barrels week over week to settle at 27.807 million barrels. Regional inventories remained constrained on an annual basis, sitting 1.513 million barrels below levels recorded during the same period in 2025.
The inventory decline occurred despite a sharp recovery in waterborne arrivals. Gasoline imports into the West Coast jumped by 44,000 b/d week over week to average 96,000 b/d, nearly doubling from the prior week's 52,000 b/d. On a year-over-year basis, incoming foreign shipments were essentially flat, edging up 1,000 b/d.
Regional output also posted a modest improvement. Finished gasoline production rose by 10,000 b/d on the week to average 1.333 million b/d, although it remained 81,000 b/d lower than year-ago volumes.
Meanwhile, top-line refinery activity pulled back slightly from recent highs. West Coast refinery utilization dipped 2.0 percentage points week over week to 93.2%. However,

operational throughput remained exceptionally strong compared to 2025, sitting a massive 15.9 percentage points higher year over year, underscoring robust crude processing across Pacific Coast facilities.
Overall, the modest stock draw highlights continued tightness across the West Coast market, where an influx of waterborne cargoes and higher finished production were absorbed by steady prompt demand, keeping stockpiles pinned below 28 million barrels.
Platts, a part of S&P Global Energy, assessed San Francisco regular CARBOB at NYMEX November RBOB futures plus 48 cents/gal and Los Angeles CARBOB at futures plus 44 cents/gal on Oct. 15.
-- Julia Martinez
Midwest gasoline stocks rebound from record low amid an output surge
Inventories rise 816,000 barrels week over week
Refinery utilization hits 89.7% capacity
Imports reach a record high for October
Midwest gasoline inventories in the US rebounded from a record low in the week ended Oct. 9 as refinery utilization climbed for a second consecutive week and imports surged to a record for the period, according to data the US Energy Information Administration published Oct. 15.
Stocks rose by 816,000 barrels to 42.226 million barrels, partially recovering from an all-time low recorded the prior week, although inventories remained more than 3 million barrels below the five-year seasonal average for the second week of October, according to S&P Global CERA analyst data.
Despite the week-on-week improvement, the overall gasoline inventory picture in PADD 2 remains strained. Total gasoline stocks closed shy of the five-year average of about 45.5 million barrels by more than 3 million barrels for the second week of October, according to S&P Global CERA analyst data. In the prior week, when stocks hit their record low in EIA records dating back to 1990, the deficit to the five-year average stood at 4.5 million barrels -- meaning the gap narrowed by about 1.5 million barrels week over week, but remains historically wide for this time of year.
Midwest refinery utilization rose 1.5 percentage points to 89.7% of operable capacity, its highest level since the week ended Sept. 4, when it reached 100%, according to the EIA data. Net production of finished motor gasoline climbed 15,000 barrels/day to 2.536 million b/d, rising for the third consecutive week and approaching -- but not yet matching -- the Sept. 4 high of 2.541 million b/d. The latest production figure closely tracked the five-year seasonal average, which closed marginally higher at 2.539 million b/d, according to the EIA data.
Gasoline blending components stocks also recovered sharply, jumping by 833,000 barrels to 38.316 million barrels, shifting from a more-than-10-year low to a three-week high.

The Buckeye Complex (BCX) storage hub saw its CBOB differential drop 12 cents to close flat to the NYMEX November RBOB futures contract, while the West Shore/Badger CBOB differential declined 9.75 cents to futures plus 2.25 cents/gal, according to Platts assessments.
On an outright basis, BCX CBOB closed at $3.1802/gal, Wolverine at $3.2102/gal, and West Shore/Badger at $3.2027/gal -- all their lowest levels since Sept. 29.
Farther south in Oklahoma, Group 3 regular suboctane gasoline also weakened on the day, although the differential held relatively steady with a marginal decline of 0.25 cent to futures plus 19.75 cents/gallon, according to Platts assessments. The heavier drag came from the broader NYMEX sell-off, which pulled the outright price down 8.18 cents/gallon to $3.3155/gallon -- the lowest outright price recorded for the grade since Sept. 30.

Japan raises fuel subsidies by 1.4% for Oct 15-21 on stronger crude
Subsidy up 60 Japanese sen/liter at ¥43/liter
Gasoline pump prices fall to ¥170.10/liter

Japan raised its fuel subsidy for oil products to ¥43/liter (27 cents/liter) for the week of Oct. 15-21, from ¥42.40/liter the

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

19

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 15, 2026

previous week, amid stronger crude oil prices, the government said Oct. 15.
The government calculates the weekly subsidy using data that includes the average retail gasoline price, as surveyed every Monday, crude oil prices and other factors. It provides a full subsidy for the portion exceeding ¥170/liter ($1.08/liter).
The national average retail price for regular gasoline fell 10 Japanese sen/liter week over week to ¥170.10/liter as of Oct. 13, according to the Oil Information Center of the Institute of Energy Economics, Japan.
The Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry used Nikkei Dubai as the benchmark for calculating subsidies from Jan. 27, 2022, to March 18, 2026, and ICE Brent from March 19 to June 3.
METI informed trading houses and wholesalers in a May 27 email, seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, that the subsidy amount would be calculated using Platts Dubai as the benchmark, starting with payments made June 4.
The Platts front-month cash Dubai assessment rose 0.2% week over week to $108.10/barrel over Oct. 6-12.
In March, due to an increase in crude oil prices driven by the Middle East conflict, the government reinstated gasoline subsidies to maintain the retail price of regular gasoline at ¥170/ liter after ending them on Dec. 30, 2025, according to documents released by METI on March 11.
A fixed subsidy of ¥5/liter was provided for kerosene and fuel oil and was scheduled to end March 31. However, the government has been providing these fuels with the same subsidy as gasoline since March 19.
For gasoil, the provisional tax rate of ¥17.10/liter was abolished April 1, according to METI. To prevent demand fluctuations caused by the price drop from the tax abolition, METI said Nov. 7, 2025, that it would provide a subsidy of ¥17.10/ liter, equal to the provisional tax rate, from Nov. 27, 2025, to March 31, 2026.
Since March 19, when the gasoline subsidy was reinstated, gasoil received a subsidy of ¥17.10/liter plus the same amount as gasoline. From April 1, the gasoil subsidy was set at the same level as the gasoline subsidy, according to METI.
Japan launched the subsidy program for gasoline, kerosene, gasoil and fuel oil in January 2022 to mitigate the impact of high oil prices on the economy as it recovered from the pandemic. In April 2022, the program was expanded to include jet fuel and LPG.
Refiners use the subsidy to curb increases in the weekly wholesale prices of oil products, while trading houses deduct it from the selling prices of imported oil products.
The Platts-assessed average prices across the Chiba, Kanagawa, Chukyo, and Hanshin regions on Oct. 15 are as follows: gasoline at ¥99,550/kl ($100.20/barrel), kerosene at ¥120,250/ kl, gasoil at ¥100,700/kl, low-sulfur A-fuel oil at ¥100,200/kl and A-fuel oil at ¥99,700/kl.
-- Hiroyuki Koshoji

Platts US Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
Gulf Coast premium grades plunge to June lows
Midwest inventories rise 816,000 barrels
West Coast stocks fall for second week straight
US gasoline markets weakened broadly Oct. 15, with Gulf Coast premium grades plunging, Chicago-area prices hitting more than two-week lows and differentials slipping in Los Angeles and Portland, while New York Harbor was comparatively steady. The declines coincided with inventory builds on the Gulf Coast and in the Midwest, even as East and West Coast stocks edged lower.

Gulf Coast US Gulf Coast gasoline premium grade gasoline differentials
dipped sharply Oct. 15, bringing regional octane values to their lowest level since late June amid a substantial build in USGC gasoline inventories.
Platts assessed the benchmark CBOB 87 octane A-grade at a 1.5 cent/gal discount to NYMEX December RBOB futures, 3 cents weaker from the previous close. CBOB 93 D-grade, meanwhile, dipped further to a 27.50 cent/gal premium, a sharp 16.50 cents weaker day over day.
The move brought regional octane values to 3.766 cents/antiknock index unit, 1.75 cents down on the day to the lowest level assessed for the indicator since June 30.
The weakness in premium grades coincided with a steep build in USGC gasoline inventories, posting a 3.04 million-barrel injection in the week ended Oct. 9 to total 78.08 million barrels, US Energy Information data showed. Present levels are the highest stocks in the Gulf Coast region since the week ended Aug. 14, though totals remain on the low end of the five-year average range.
The large build follows two consecutive withdrawals over the past two weeks, which combined brought inventories about 2.6 million barrels lower to a nine-year low of 75.04 million barrels.
Atlantic Coast On the Atlantic Coast, differentials for New York Harbor barges
remained stable as outright prices inched up. Platts assessed 13.5 RVP RBOB NYH barges at NYMEX
November RBOB futures plus 3.75 cents/gallon, down 2.50 cents from the last close. Outright prices fell 10.43 cents to $3.2177/gal.
US East Coast gasoline inventories ticked down 186,000 barrels week over week to 53.557 million barrels for the week ended Oct. 9, widening the regional deficit to nearly 2.98 million barrels below year-ago levels, EIA data showed. The modest draw was driven by a 52,000 b/d drop in waterborne imports to 370,000 b/d, which outweighed an uptick in domestic refining activity.
Atlantic Coast refinery run rates rebounded 3.2 percentage points on the week to 81.1%, though utilization remained heavily depressed year over year by 12.3 percentage points amid seasonal maintenance. Regional finished gasoline production rose 26,000 b/d week over week to 3.146 million b/d but trailed 2025 volumes by 72,000 b/d, failing to fully offset the decline in foreign cargo arrivals.

than two weeks on Oct. 15 as Platts began assessing October Cycle 3 barrels across the Chicago complex, while Oklahoma Group 3 regular suboctane's outright price also sank to a twoweek low amid weakness in NYMEX November RBOB futures.

Jet
Jet fuel-diesel regrade widens; market cautious on yield switching
Spread reaches a near six-month high Oct. 7
Refineries delay yield switching despite premium
Tighter diesel supply makes switching challenging
The spread between the European jet fuel FOB FARAG bargeto-Brent crude crack and the ULSD FOB barge-to-Brent crude crack, known as the regrade, remained strong at $13.17/b on Oct. 14 after hitting a near six-month high a week earlier, supported by tighter jet fuel inventories in Europe.
The regrade remained well above historical norms, compared

with flat levels a year earlier and a discount of about 22 cents/b two years ago, according to Platts data.
On Oct. 7, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the regrade at $16.45/b, marking the highest level seen since April 24, when it reached $23.04/b.
While a wider regrade would typically signal yield switching at refineries, traders said the market remained cautious.
"The diesel crunch at the moment makes it ... difficult to switch to jet fuel," a Europe-based jet fuel trader said. "But there has been a stronger regrade for a few weeks. Refineries are taking time to plan a change."
The trader added that higher jet fuel yields were not expected to reach the market until November.
"I think they will switch back over to jet fuel," a European diesel trader said. "That could [also] support US diesel cracks."
Another European jet fuel trader said: "The [jet fuel] market cannot tighten further. We will get more production soon."
Diesel typically commands a premium during AugustSeptember, supported by harvest-season demand and a pickup in trucking activity, but the market generally slows in October as seasonal demand wanes.
"It is typical at this time of year [for jet fuel] to go positive if it was negative," the diesel trader said.
That said, the regrade also favored diesel production at the same point in the previous two years, although it came close to flipping toward jet fuel on both occasions.
Jet fuel fundamentals supported a positive regrade, with tighter market conditions driven by lower inventories.
Jet fuel stocks in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp refining hub rebounded 9.6% week over week to 479,000 metric tons on Oct. 8, reversing the previous week's draw, according to data from Insights Global. However, inventories remained 55% lower than a year earlier.
"We are seeing strength in jet fuel driven by high cash differentials," the second European jet fuel trader said. "Jet fuel availability is low; higher storage costs mean we are seeing more product being sold out of storage."
-- Aruni Sunil, Patrick Mcallister, Sophia Aung, Oskar Leskow

Platts Asia and Middle Eastern Jet Daily Market Analysis
Middle East tensions pressure discount within Gulf
Australia's August jet imports drop 22% MOM
The Asian jet fuel/kerosene market firmed Oct. 15, reflected by gains in the regrade, amid muted spot offers from South Korea.
A broker pegged the balance October and front-month November regrade swap spreads, the premium of jet fuel over 10 ppm sulfur gasoil, at plus $2.60/barrel and plus $1.70/b, respectively, in midafternoon Asia trading Oct. 15. In comparison, Platts assessed the M0 and M1 regrade swap spreads at plus $1.91/b and plus $1.50/b, respectively, at the Oct. 14 Asian close.
"The prompt Chinese October barrels filled some of the demand, even if they are offers that resumed," a Singapore-based jet fuel trader said.
"Initial shorts are gone I think ... Recent events feel like an escalation so it might lead to another round of tightening, and maybe that's what people are kind of expecting," said a South Korean jet fuel trader.
The spot differential for Middle East jet fuel/kerosene cargoes loading within the Gulf region widened 50 cents/b day over day to minus $17/b Oct. 14, pressured by escalating hostilities.
An LR1 cargo for early November delivery to Jebel Ali was heard to have traded at a spot discount of about minus $5-$6/b to the Mean of Platts Arab Gulf jet fuel/kerosene assessments on a delivered basis, according to regional trade sources, including one with close knowledge of the matter. Trade sources estimated this to be equivalent to about minus $17-$18/b, FOB.
Fresh attacks in the Middle East have once again driven regional spot prices inside and outside the Gulf to diverge, as the risk of crossing the Strait of Hormuz intensifies, sources said. Platts assessed the spot premium for Middle East jet fuel/ kerosene cargoes loading outside the Gulf region at a premium of $19/b Oct. 14, up 25 cents/b day over day and $1/b week over week.
In data news, Australia imported 3.37 million barrels of jet fuel in August, down 22.02% month over month and 31.64% year over year, as nearly all suppliers reduced shipments, the latest preliminary data from the Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water showed Oct. 15.
Inflows from South Korea fell 19.07% month over month and 41.33% year over year to 1.47 million barrels in August, while those

from Taiwan dropped 0.11% month over month and 3.50% year over year to 589,984 barrels.
Imports from Singapore and Malaysia nearly halved month over month to 362,922 barrels and 318,893 barrels, respectively, in August. On a year-over-year basis, volumes from Singapore and Malaysia fell 43.98% and 33.81%, respectively.
Only volumes from China rose month over month, rebounding to 623,949 barrels in August from zero in July. However, imports from China remained 7.72% lower year over year.
As of Oct. 6, Australia's petroleum industry held 2.871 billion liters of gasoil, 811 million liters of kerosene and 1.823 billion liters of gasoline under its Minimum Stockholding Obligation, DCCEEW data released Oct. 9 showed.
This equated to 31 days of cover for diesel, 30 days for kerosene and 43 days for gasoline -- 27%, 20% and 106% above current MSO requirements, respectively.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Shu ling Lee

AUSTRALIA DATA: Middle distillate imports fall 11% MOM in August
Gasoil imports down 8% MOM to 14.44 mil barrels
Jet fuel imports down 22% MOM to 3.37 mil barrels
Australia's middle distillate imports fell 11.3% month over month and 16.3% year over year to 17.82 million barrels in August, the latest preliminary data from the Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water showed Oct. 15.
The country's gasoil imports fell 8.44% month over month and 11.74% year over year to 14.44 million barrels in August. Total imports in the first eight months of 2026 inched 3.39% higher year over year to 129.01 million barrels, the data showed.
The decline came despite higher imports from South Korea, the top supplier, which rose 5.4% month over month and 8.66% year over year to 6.15 million barrels.
Inflows from Singapore edged 3.13% lower month over month but rose 10.9% year over year to 2.41 million barrels.
Imports from Taiwan fell 25.58% month over month and 0.11% year over year to 1.76 million barrels in August, while those from Malaysia fell 62.96% month over month and and 81.60% year over year to 603,822 barrels.
There were no imports from India in August, compared with 712,635 barrels in July and 686,847 barrels in August 2025.
Shipments from Brunei rose 8.99% month over month and 116.28% year over year to 2 million barrels in August. Imports from Japan rose 50.83% month over month but fell 40.65% year over year to 916,425 barrels.
Notably, arrivals from China doubled month over month and year over year to 606,967 barrels in August.
The Platts benchmark outright price for FOB Singapore 10 parts per million sulfur gasoil averaged $156.47/barrel in August, up from $142.37/b in July and $126.12/b in June. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
Jet fuel imports slide Australia imported 3.37 million barrels of jet fuel in August,
down 22.02% month over month and 31.64% year over year, as nearly all suppliers reduced shipments, DCCEEW data showed.
